People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I’ve been worrying more than I’d like to admit. It’s not that I haven’t tried to be sensible about things—I make lists, I check dates, I try to make sure I’ve done what I reasonably can—but once the worry starts, I can’t always seem to turn it off. I know that probably sounds a little silly, but it’s been hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: What are you usually worrying about?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s a bit of everything, really—not dramatic things, necessarily. My mind will go from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter’s travel plans, to wondering if the gutters on the house need fixing. And then these small things start to feel as though, if I don’t keep track of them properly, they could turn into something much bigger. I know that may sound like ordinary worrying, but it sort of… keeps roaming.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not avoiding completely, no… I mean, I still do the garden and I go to book club, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I just sometimes feel better if I’ve checked enough to reassure myself. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Yes… I suppose they are repetitive and unwanted, though I’m not sure I’d call them intrusive in the way some people mean it.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I’ve been worrying more than I’d like to admit. It’s not that I haven’t tried to be sensible about things—I make lists, I check dates, I try to make sure I’ve done what I reasonably can—but once the worry starts, I can’t always seem to turn it off. I know that probably sounds a little silly, but it’s been hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: What are you usually worrying about?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s a bit of everything, really—not dramatic things, necessarily. My mind will go from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter’s travel plans, to wondering if the gutters on the house need fixing. And then these small things start to feel as though, if I don’t keep track of them properly, they could turn into something much bigger. I know that may sound like ordinary worrying, but it sort of… keeps roaming.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not avoiding completely, no… I mean, I still do the garden and I go to book club, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I have noticed I’m arriving less prepared than I used to, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down certain parts because it just feels harder to manage now. I suppose that counts a little, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your body when you're worrying, like tension or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit, I suppose. Nothing terribly dramatic, but I do notice I can feel quite wound up, even just sitting in my own living room. I’ll be on the phone and realize I’m tapping my fingers, or I’ll keep getting up to straighten something that probably doesn’t need straightening. It’s as if sitting quietly makes me a little jumpy. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I have moments where I feel a bit flat or worn out, but… I don’t think of myself as down, exactly. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose the worries do go round and round a bit. I don’t mean anything strange, just ordinary things really — dates, arrangements, whether I’ve forgotten something. I’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you’ve checked it, you’ve done what you can,” but it doesn’t always switch off. It keeps coming back even when I know, logically, there’s probably nothing more to do. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or activities that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not avoid completely, no… I do still keep up with things, like the garden and my book club, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I suppose I’m not quite as on top of them as I used to be.\n\nWith the book club, I might arrive less prepared than I’d like, and with the exhibition planning I’ve been helping with, I’ve started saying no to certain parts of it. It just feels a bit harder to manage than it used to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive checks or actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as preventing something bad, exactly. It’s more… I feel unsettled if I don’t check, like I might have missed something. But I don’t have a very clear disaster in mind, if that makes sense. Maybe I’m making too fine a distinction there?\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted, that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. It’s more that once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to turn it off properly. I’ll tell myself I’ve made the list, checked the dates, done everything reasonably possible… but the thought still comes back and nags at me.\n\nSo yes, unwanted in that sense. I do try to push it aside, but it doesn’t always listen, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or repeating certain actions helps to reduce your worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure. I suppose in the moment it can make me feel a bit more settled, but I don’t know that it really reduces the worry in any lasting way.\n\nIt’s more like… if I check something, I can tell myself I’ve done what I’m supposed to do. But then sometimes the uneasiness is still there anyway. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or actions in a specific way to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a very fixed or ritual kind of way. It’s more that I like to feel I’ve looked properly, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t have a particular order I must do things in, or a number of times. I just sometimes feel better if I’ve checked enough to reassure myself. I hope that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are repetitive, unwanted, and intrusive, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose they are repetitive and unwanted, though I’m not sure I’d call them intrusive in the way some people mean it.\n\nIt’s more that once the worry starts, I can’t seem to switch it off. Even if I’ve made a list, checked the dates, and done what I reasonably can, my mind still keeps circling back to it. I do try to push it aside and tell myself it’s handled, but it doesn’t always work. Is that what you’re asking about?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I know that may sound like ordinary worrying, but it sort of… keeps roaming.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not avoiding completely, no… I mean, I still do the garden and I go to book club, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I have noticed I’m arriving less prepared than I used to, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down certain parts because it just feels harder to manage now. I suppose that counts a little, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your body when you're worrying, like tension or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit, I suppose. Nothing terribly dramatic, but I do notice I can feel quite wound up, even just sitting in my own living room. I’ll be on the phone and realize I’m tapping my fingers, or I’ll keep getting up to straighten something that probably doesn’t need straightening. It’s as if sitting quietly makes me a little jumpy. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I have moments where I feel a bit flat or worn out, but… I don’t think of myself as down, exactly. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose the worries do go round and round a bit. I don’t mean anything strange, just ordinary things really — dates, arrangements, whether I’ve forgotten something. I’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you’ve checked it, you’ve done what you can,” but it doesn’t always switch off. It keeps coming back even when I know, logically, there’s probably nothing more to do. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or activities that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not avoid completely, no… I do still keep up with things, like the garden and my book club, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I suppose I’m not quite as on top of them as I used to be.\n\nWith the book club, I might arrive less prepared than I’d like, and with the exhibition planning I’ve been helping with, I’ve started saying no to certain parts of it. It just feels a bit harder to manage than it used to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive checks or actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as preventing something bad, exactly. It’s more… I feel unsettled if I don’t check, like I might have missed something. But I don’t have a very clear disaster in mind, if that makes sense. Maybe I’m making too fine a distinction there?\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted, that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. It’s more that once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to turn it off properly. I’ll tell myself I’ve made the list, checked the dates, done everything reasonably possible… but the thought still comes back and nags at me.\n\nSo yes, unwanted in that sense. I do try to push it aside, but it doesn’t always listen, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or repeating certain actions helps to reduce your worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure. I suppose in the moment it can make me feel a bit more settled, but I don’t know that it really reduces the worry in any lasting way.\n\nIt’s more like… if I check something, I can tell myself I’ve done what I’m supposed to do. But then sometimes the uneasiness is still there anyway. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or actions in a specific way to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a very fixed or ritual kind of way. It’s more that I like to feel I’ve looked properly, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t have a particular order I must do things in, or a number of times. I just sometimes feel better if I’ve checked enough to reassure myself. I hope that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are repetitive, unwanted, and intrusive, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose they are repetitive and unwanted, though I’m not sure I’d call them intrusive in the way some people mean it.\n\nIt’s more that once the worry starts, I can’t seem to switch it off. Even if I’ve made a list, checked the dates, and done what I reasonably can, my mind still keeps circling back to it. I do try to push it aside and tell myself it’s handled, but it doesn’t always work. Is that what you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to overstate it. I do still keep up with things like the garden and my book club, so it’s not as if I’ve stopped everything.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m sometimes arriving less prepared than I’d like, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down some parts of it because it feels harder to manage than it used to. I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not coping at all… but yes, I suppose it is getting in the way somewhat.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It does move around, I think. It’s not just one thing, unfortunately… though I try not to make too much of it.

I suppose in the moment it can make me feel a bit more settled, but I don’t know that it really reduces the worry in any lasting way.\n\nIt’s more like… if I check something, I can tell myself I’ve done what I’m supposed to do. But then sometimes the uneasiness is still there anyway. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or actions in a specific way to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a very fixed or ritual kind of way. It’s more that I like to feel I’ve looked properly, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t have a particular order I must do things in, or a number of times. I just sometimes feel better if I’ve checked enough to reassure myself. I hope that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are repetitive, unwanted, and intrusive, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose they are repetitive and unwanted, though I’m not sure I’d call them intrusive in the way some people mean it.\n\nIt’s more that once the worry starts, I can’t seem to switch it off. Even if I’ve made a list, checked the dates, and done what I reasonably can, my mind still keeps circling back to it. I do try to push it aside and tell myself it’s handled, but it doesn’t always work. Is that what you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to overstate it. I do still keep up with things like the garden and my book club, so it’s not as if I’ve stopped everything.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m sometimes arriving less prepared than I’d like, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down some parts of it because it feels harder to manage than it used to. I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not coping at all… but yes, I suppose it is getting in the way somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one particular area of your life or does it seem to jump around to different things?\n\nPatient: It does move around, I think. It’s not just one thing, unfortunately… though I try not to make too much of it.\n\nMy mind might start with the exhibition schedule, and then before long I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, and then something ordinary like whether the house gutters need fixing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I’ve been worrying more than I’d like to admit. It’s not that I haven’t tried to be sensible about things—I make lists, I check dates, I try to make sure I’ve done what I reasonably can—but once the worry starts, I can’t always seem to turn it off. I know that probably sounds a little silly, but it’s been hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: What are you usually worrying about?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s a bit of everything, really—not dramatic things, necessarily. My mind will go from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter’s travel plans, to wondering if the gutters on the house need fixing. And then these small things start to feel as though, if I don’t keep track of them properly, they could turn into something much bigger. I know that may sound like ordinary worrying, but it sort of… keeps roaming.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not avoiding completely, no… I mean, I still do the garden and I go to book club, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I have noticed I’m arriving less prepared than I used to, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down certain parts because it just feels harder to manage now. I suppose that counts a little, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your body when you're worrying, like tension or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit, I suppose. Nothing terribly dramatic, but I do notice I can feel quite wound up, even just sitting in my own living room. I’ll be on the phone and realize I’m tapping my fingers, or I’ll keep getting up to straighten something that probably doesn’t need straightening. It’s as if sitting quietly makes me a little jumpy. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I have moments where I feel a bit flat or worn out, but… I don’t think of myself as down, exactly. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose the worries do go round and round a bit. I don’t mean anything strange, just ordinary things really — dates, arrangements, whether I’ve forgotten something. I’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you’ve checked it, you’ve done what you can,” but it doesn’t always switch off. It keeps coming back even when I know, logically, there’s probably nothing more to do. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or activities that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not avoid completely, no… I do still keep up with things, like the garden and my book club, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I suppose I’m not quite as on top of them as I used to be.\n\nWith the book club, I might arrive less prepared than I’d like, and with the exhibition planning I’ve been helping with, I’ve started saying no to certain parts of it. It just feels a bit harder to manage than it used to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive checks or actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as preventing something bad, exactly. It’s more… I feel unsettled if I don’t check, like I might have missed something. But I don’t have a very clear disaster in mind, if that makes sense. Maybe I’m making too fine a distinction there?\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted, that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. It’s more that once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to turn it off properly. I’ll tell myself I’ve made the list, checked the dates, done everything reasonably possible… but the thought still comes back and nags at me.\n\nSo yes, unwanted in that sense. I do try to push it aside, but it doesn’t always listen, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or repeating certain actions helps to reduce your worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure. I suppose in the moment it can make me feel a bit more settled, but I don’t know that it really reduces the worry in any lasting way.\n\nIt’s more like… if I check something, I can tell myself I’ve done what I’m supposed to do. But then sometimes the uneasiness is still there anyway. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or actions in a specific way to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a very fixed or ritual kind of way. It’s more that I like to feel I’ve looked properly, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t have a particular order I must do things in, or a number of times. I just sometimes feel better if I’ve checked enough to reassure myself. I hope that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are repetitive, unwanted, and intrusive, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose they are repetitive and unwanted, though I’m not sure I’d call them intrusive in the way some people mean it.\n\nIt’s more that once the worry starts, I can’t seem to switch it off. Even if I’ve made a list, checked the dates, and done what I reasonably can, my mind still keeps circling back to it. I do try to push it aside and tell myself it’s handled, but it doesn’t always work. Is that what you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to overstate it. I do still keep up with things like the garden and my book club, so it’s not as if I’ve stopped everything.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m sometimes arriving less prepared than I’d like, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down some parts of it because it feels harder to manage than it used to. I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not coping at all… but yes, I suppose it is getting in the way somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one particular area of your life or does it seem to jump around to different things?\n\nPatient: It does move around, I think. It’s not just one thing, unfortunately… though I try not to make too much of it.\n\nMy mind might start with the exhibition schedule, and then before long I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, and then something ordinary like whether the house gutters need fixing. Small things can start to feel as if they might turn into something much bigger if I don’t keep on top of them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to perform certain routines or rituals in order to feel okay or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not rituals, really.\n\nI mean, I do like to be organized, and I may check a note or make sure I’ve written something down, but I wouldn’t say I have to do a particular routine to stop something bad happening. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I’ve been worrying more than I’d like to admit. It’s not that I haven’t tried to be sensible about things—I make lists, I check dates, I try to make sure I’ve done what I reasonably can—but once the worry starts, I can’t always seem to turn it off. I know that probably sounds a little silly, but it’s been hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: What are you usually worrying about?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s a bit of everything, really—not dramatic things, necessarily. My mind will go from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter’s travel plans, to wondering if the gutters on the house need fixing. And then these small things start to feel as though, if I don’t keep track of them properly, they could turn into something much bigger. I know that may sound like ordinary worrying, but it sort of… keeps roaming.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or situations because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not avoiding completely, no… I mean, I still do the garden and I go to book club, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I have noticed I’m arriving less prepared than I used to, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down certain parts because it just feels harder to manage now. I suppose that counts a little, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your body when you're worrying, like tension or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit, I suppose. Nothing terribly dramatic, but I do notice I can feel quite wound up, even just sitting in my own living room. I’ll be on the phone and realize I’m tapping my fingers, or I’ll keep getting up to straighten something that probably doesn’t need straightening. It’s as if sitting quietly makes me a little jumpy. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I have moments where I feel a bit flat or worn out, but… I don’t think of myself as down, exactly. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose the worries do go round and round a bit. I don’t mean anything strange, just ordinary things really — dates, arrangements, whether I’ve forgotten something. I’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you’ve checked it, you’ve done what you can,” but it doesn’t always switch off. It keeps coming back even when I know, logically, there’s probably nothing more to do. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or activities that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not avoid completely, no… I do still keep up with things, like the garden and my book club, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I suppose I’m not quite as on top of them as I used to be.\n\nWith the book club, I might arrive less prepared than I’d like, and with the exhibition planning I’ve been helping with, I’ve started saying no to certain parts of it. It just feels a bit harder to manage than it used to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive checks or actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as preventing something bad, exactly. It’s more… I feel unsettled if I don’t check, like I might have missed something. But I don’t have a very clear disaster in mind, if that makes sense. Maybe I’m making too fine a distinction there?\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted, that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. It’s more that once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to turn it off properly. I’ll tell myself I’ve made the list, checked the dates, done everything reasonably possible… but the thought still comes back and nags at me.\n\nSo yes, unwanted in that sense. I do try to push it aside, but it doesn’t always listen, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or repeating certain actions helps to reduce your worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure. I suppose in the moment it can make me feel a bit more settled, but I don’t know that it really reduces the worry in any lasting way.\n\nIt’s more like… if I check something, I can tell myself I’ve done what I’m supposed to do. But then sometimes the uneasiness is still there anyway. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or actions in a specific way to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a very fixed or ritual kind of way. It’s more that I like to feel I’ve looked properly, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t have a particular order I must do things in, or a number of times. I just sometimes feel better if I’ve checked enough to reassure myself. I hope that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are repetitive, unwanted, and intrusive, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose they are repetitive and unwanted, though I’m not sure I’d call them intrusive in the way some people mean it.\n\nIt’s more that once the worry starts, I can’t seem to switch it off. Even if I’ve made a list, checked the dates, and done what I reasonably can, my mind still keeps circling back to it. I do try to push it aside and tell myself it’s handled, but it doesn’t always work. Is that what you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to overstate it. I do still keep up with things like the garden and my book club, so it’s not as if I’ve stopped everything.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m sometimes arriving less prepared than I’d like, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down some parts of it because it feels harder to manage than it used to. I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not coping at all… but yes, I suppose it is getting in the way somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one particular area of your life or does it seem to jump around to different things?\n\nPatient: It does move around, I think. It’s not just one thing, unfortunately… though I try not to make too much of it.\n\nMy mind might start with the exhibition schedule, and then before long I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, and then something ordinary like whether the house gutters need fixing. Small things can start to feel as if they might turn into something much bigger if I don’t keep on top of them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to perform certain routines or rituals in order to feel okay or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not rituals, really.\n\nI mean, I do like to be organized, and I may check a note or make sure I’ve written something down, but I wouldn’t say I have to do a particular routine to stop something bad happening. At least, I haven’t noticed anything like that. Does that sound like the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular thing that you worry about more than others, or does it seem to be a mix of different things?\n\nPatient: It’s more of a mix, I think. I wish I could say it was just one thing, but my mind does tend to roam.\n\nIt might be the exhibition schedule at first, and then I’m suddenly thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, or whether the gutters need fixing. They’re not always enormous things, but they can start to feel as though, if I don’t keep track of them, they might unravel into something bigger. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I’ve been worrying more than I’d like to admit. It’s not that I haven’t tried to be sensible about things—I make lists, I check dates, I try to make sure I’ve done what I reasonably can—but once the worry starts, I can’t always seem to turn it off. I know that probably sounds a little silly, but it’s been hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: What are you usually worrying about?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s a bit of everything, really—not dramatic things, necessarily. My mind will go from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter’s travel plans, to wondering if the gutters on the house need fixing. And then these small things start to feel as though, if I don’t keep track of them properly, they could turn into something much bigger. I suppose that counts a little, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your body when you're worrying, like tension or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit, I suppose. Nothing terribly dramatic, but I do notice I can feel quite wound up, even just sitting in my own living room. I mean, I have moments where I feel a bit flat or worn out, but… I don’t think of myself as down, exactly. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose the worries do go round and round a bit. I don’t mean anything strange, just ordinary things really — dates, arrangements, whether I’ve forgotten something. I’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you’ve checked it, you’ve done what you can,” but it doesn’t always switch off. It keeps coming back even when I know, logically, there’s probably nothing more to do. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or activities that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not avoid completely, no… I do still keep up with things, like the garden and my book club, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I suppose I’m not quite as on top of them as I used to be.\n\nWith the book club, I might arrive less prepared than I’d like, and with the exhibition planning I’ve been helping with, I’ve started saying no to certain parts of it. It just feels a bit harder to manage than it used to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive checks or actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as preventing something bad, exactly. It’s more… I feel unsettled if I don’t check, like I might have missed something. But I don’t have a very clear disaster in mind, if that makes sense. Maybe I’m making too fine a distinction there?\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted, that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. It’s more that once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to turn it off properly. I’ll tell myself I’ve made the list, checked the dates, done everything reasonably possible… but the thought still comes back and nags at me.\n\nSo yes, unwanted in that sense. I do try to push it aside, but it doesn’t always listen, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or repeating certain actions helps to reduce your worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure. I suppose in the moment it can make me feel a bit more settled, but I don’t know that it really reduces the worry in any lasting way.\n\nIt’s more like… if I check something, I can tell myself I’ve done what I’m supposed to do. But then sometimes the uneasiness is still there anyway. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or actions in a specific way to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a very fixed or ritual kind of way. It’s more that I like to feel I’ve looked properly, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t have a particular order I must do things in, or a number of times. I just sometimes feel better if I’ve checked enough to reassure myself. I hope that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are repetitive, unwanted, and intrusive, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose they are repetitive and unwanted, though I’m not sure I’d call them intrusive in the way some people mean it.\n\nIt’s more that once the worry starts, I can’t seem to switch it off. Even if I’ve made a list, checked the dates, and done what I reasonably can, my mind still keeps circling back to it. I do try to push it aside and tell myself it’s handled, but it doesn’t always work. Is that what you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to overstate it. I do still keep up with things like the garden and my book club, so it’s not as if I’ve stopped everything.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m sometimes arriving less prepared than I’d like, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down some parts of it because it feels harder to manage than it used to. I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not coping at all… but yes, I suppose it is getting in the way somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one particular area of your life or does it seem to jump around to different things?\n\nPatient: It does move around, I think. It’s not just one thing, unfortunately… though I try not to make too much of it.\n\nMy mind might start with the exhibition schedule, and then before long I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, and then something ordinary like whether the house gutters need fixing. Small things can start to feel as if they might turn into something much bigger if I don’t keep on top of them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to perform certain routines or rituals in order to feel okay or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not rituals, really.\n\nI mean, I do like to be organized, and I may check a note or make sure I’ve written something down, but I wouldn’t say I have to do a particular routine to stop something bad happening. At least, I haven’t noticed anything like that. Does that sound like the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular thing that you worry about more than others, or does it seem to be a mix of different things?\n\nPatient: It’s more of a mix, I think. I wish I could say it was just one thing, but my mind does tend to roam.\n\nIt might be the exhibition schedule at first, and then I’m suddenly thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, or whether the gutters need fixing. They’re not always enormous things, but they can start to feel as though, if I don’t keep track of them, they might unravel into something bigger. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you a lot of distress or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, though I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nI’m still keeping up with things like the garden and my book club, so it’s not as if I’ve stopped everything. But I have noticed I’m arriving less prepared than I used to, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down certain parts because they feel harder to manage than they should.\n\nIt’s uncomfortable, I suppose… more than I’d like to admit. Does that answer it properly?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I suppose it shows up most around things I feel responsible for keeping in order.

The exhibition schedule is a big one, but it doesn’t stay there. My mind can move from that to my daughter’s travel plans, and then to something like whether the house gutters need fixing. I don’t mean anything strange, just ordinary things really — dates, arrangements, whether I’ve forgotten something. But I suppose I’m not quite as on top of them as I used to be.\n\nWith the book club, I might arrive less prepared than I’d like, and with the exhibition planning I’ve been helping with, I’ve started saying no to certain parts of it. It just feels a bit harder to manage than it used to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive checks or actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as preventing something bad, exactly. It’s more… I feel unsettled if I don’t check, like I might have missed something. But I don’t have a very clear disaster in mind, if that makes sense. Maybe I’m making too fine a distinction there?\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted, that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. It’s more that once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to turn it off properly. I’ll tell myself I’ve made the list, checked the dates, done everything reasonably possible… but the thought still comes back and nags at me.\n\nSo yes, unwanted in that sense. I do try to push it aside, but it doesn’t always listen, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or repeating certain actions helps to reduce your worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure. I suppose in the moment it can make me feel a bit more settled, but I don’t know that it really reduces the worry in any lasting way.\n\nIt’s more like… if I check something, I can tell myself I’ve done what I’m supposed to do. But then sometimes the uneasiness is still there anyway. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or actions in a specific way to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a very fixed or ritual kind of way. I just sometimes feel better if I’ve checked enough to reassure myself. Even if I’ve made a list, checked the dates, and done what I reasonably can, my mind still keeps circling back to it. I do try to push it aside and tell myself it’s handled, but it doesn’t always work. Is that what you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to overstate it. I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not coping at all… but yes, I suppose it is getting in the way somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one particular area of your life or does it seem to jump around to different things?\n\nPatient: It does move around, I think. It’s not just one thing, unfortunately… though I try not to make too much of it.\n\nMy mind might start with the exhibition schedule, and then before long I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, and then something ordinary like whether the house gutters need fixing. Small things can start to feel as if they might turn into something much bigger if I don’t keep on top of them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to perform certain routines or rituals in order to feel okay or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not rituals, really.\n\nI mean, I do like to be organized, and I may check a note or make sure I’ve written something down, but I wouldn’t say I have to do a particular routine to stop something bad happening. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular area of your life where you feel your worrying is most prominent?\n\nPatient: I suppose it shows up most around things I feel responsible for keeping in order.\n\nThe exhibition schedule is a big one, but it doesn’t stay there. I think it’s less one theme and more… keeping things from going wrong, if that makes sense.

My mind will start with the exhibition schedule, and then it wanders to my daughter’s travel plans, and then something ordinary like whether the gutters need fixing. Not completely, no… though I wish I could say they were.

I’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you’ve checked it, you’ve done what you can,” but it doesn’t always switch off. It keeps coming back even when I know, logically, there’s probably nothing more to do. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or activities that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Not avoid completely, no… I do still keep up with things, like the garden and my book club, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But I suppose I’m not quite as on top of them as I used to be.\n\nWith the book club, I might arrive less prepared than I’d like, and with the exhibition planning I’ve been helping with, I’ve started saying no to certain parts of it. It just feels a bit harder to manage than it used to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive checks or actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as preventing something bad, exactly. It’s more… I feel unsettled if I don’t check, like I might have missed something. But I don’t have a very clear disaster in mind, if that makes sense. Maybe I’m making too fine a distinction there?\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted, that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. It’s more that once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to turn it off properly. I’ll tell myself I’ve made the list, checked the dates, done everything reasonably possible… but the thought still comes back and nags at me.\n\nSo yes, unwanted in that sense. I do try to push it aside, but it doesn’t always listen, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or repeating certain actions helps to reduce your worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure. I suppose in the moment it can make me feel a bit more settled, but I don’t know that it really reduces the worry in any lasting way.\n\nIt’s more like… if I check something, I can tell myself I’ve done what I’m supposed to do. But then sometimes the uneasiness is still there anyway. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or actions in a specific way to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a very fixed or ritual kind of way. It’s more that I like to feel I’ve looked properly, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t have a particular order I must do things in, or a number of times. I just sometimes feel better if I’ve checked enough to reassure myself. I hope that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are repetitive, unwanted, and intrusive, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose they are repetitive and unwanted, though I’m not sure I’d call them intrusive in the way some people mean it.\n\nIt’s more that once the worry starts, I can’t seem to switch it off. Even if I’ve made a list, checked the dates, and done what I reasonably can, my mind still keeps circling back to it. I do try to push it aside and tell myself it’s handled, but it doesn’t always work. Is that what you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to overstate it. I do still keep up with things like the garden and my book club, so it’s not as if I’ve stopped everything.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m sometimes arriving less prepared than I’d like, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down some parts of it because it feels harder to manage than it used to. I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not coping at all… but yes, I suppose it is getting in the way somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one particular area of your life or does it seem to jump around to different things?\n\nPatient: It does move around, I think. It’s not just one thing, unfortunately… though I try not to make too much of it.\n\nMy mind might start with the exhibition schedule, and then before long I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, and then something ordinary like whether the house gutters need fixing. Small things can start to feel as if they might turn into something much bigger if I don’t keep on top of them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to perform certain routines or rituals in order to feel okay or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not rituals, really.\n\nI mean, I do like to be organized, and I may check a note or make sure I’ve written something down, but I wouldn’t say I have to do a particular routine to stop something bad happening. At least, I haven’t noticed anything like that. Does that sound like the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular thing that you worry about more than others, or does it seem to be a mix of different things?\n\nPatient: It’s more of a mix, I think. I wish I could say it was just one thing, but my mind does tend to roam.\n\nIt might be the exhibition schedule at first, and then I’m suddenly thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, or whether the gutters need fixing. They’re not always enormous things, but they can start to feel as though, if I don’t keep track of them, they might unravel into something bigger. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you a lot of distress or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, though I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nI’m still keeping up with things like the garden and my book club, so it’s not as if I’ve stopped everything. But I have noticed I’m arriving less prepared than I used to, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down certain parts because they feel harder to manage than they should.\n\nIt’s uncomfortable, I suppose… more than I’d like to admit. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular area of your life where you feel your worrying is most prominent?\n\nPatient: I suppose it shows up most around things I feel responsible for keeping in order.\n\nThe exhibition schedule is a big one, but it doesn’t stay there. My mind can move from that to my daughter’s travel plans, and then to something like whether the house gutters need fixing. They’re small enough things, really, but they start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t keep on top of them.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s too broad an answer, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific theme or area that most of your worries seem to center around?\n\nPatient: I think it’s less one theme and more… keeping things from going wrong, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind will start with the exhibition schedule, and then it wanders to my daughter’s travel plans, and then something ordinary like whether the gutters need fixing. They’re not necessarily dramatic things, but I can make them feel bigger in my head — like if I don’t stay on top of them, they might unravel into something more serious.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s the kind of “theme” you mean, but that seems to be the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries or thoughts are completely under your control?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no… though I wish I could say they were.\n\nI can be quite sensible about it at first — make a list, check the dates, tell myself I’ve done what I reasonably can. But once the worry has started, I can’t always seem to switch it off. It keeps circling back, even when there isn’t anything more for me to do.\n\nI know that probably sounds a bit silly, because I’m not helpless exactly… but it does feel less under my control than I’d like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
